\mu_{1}\partial_{Y|Y=0^{-}}\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell}&\mu_{0}\,\partial_{y|y=0^{+}}\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p-1,\ell}&\text{ on }\Gamma\end{array}\right.

(2.28)

Let
\mathbf{u}^{0}_{\varepsilon}:=\fsum_{p,\ell}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell}
. Summing over
(p,\ell)
(
2.28
) times
\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon
yields

{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\mu_{0}\Delta\mathbf{u}^{0}_{\varepsilon}+\omega^{2}\rho_{0}\mathbf{u}^{0}_{\varepsilon}&0&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
\mu_{1}\partial_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{u}^{0}_{\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{2}(\mu_{1}\partial_{x}^{2}+\omega^{2}\rho_{1})\mathbf{u}^{0}_{\varepsilon}&0&\text{ in }\Lambda\\[1.42262pt]
\mu_{1}\partial_{Y|Y=0^{-}}\mathbf{u}^{0}_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\,\mu_{0}\,\partial_{y|y=0^{+}}\mathbf{u}^{0}_{\varepsilon}&0&\text{ on }\Gamma\end{array}\right.

(2.29)

Let
\mathbf{v}^{0}_{\varepsilon}:=(\mathrm{id}-\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}}R)\mathbf{u}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
. Let us show that (
2.29
) implies (
2.26
). It is easy to see that
\mathrm{id}-\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}}R
maps
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)
into itself, so
\mathbf{v}^{0}_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)
. By Definition
2.1
, we have

\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{(R_{1},\dots,R_{n})\in(\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon})^{n}}R_{1}\circ\cdots\circ R_{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}}R\bigg)^{n}=\bigg(\mathrm{id}-\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}}R\bigg)^{-1}.

So
\mathbf{u}^{0}_{\varepsilon}=\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\mathbf{v}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
. Moreover, Proposition
2.3
implies
\forall R\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\setminus\{-k_{0}^{2}R_{\Delta}\},\;\Delta\circ R=0
. Hence

(\mu_{0}\Delta+\omega^{2}\rho_{0})\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle=\mu_{0}\Delta\circ(\mathrm{id}+k_{0}^{2}R_{\Delta})\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle=\mu_{0}\Delta\circ\bigg(\mathrm{id}-\sum_{R\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}}R\bigg)\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle=\mu_{0}\Delta

Similarly, one can check that
\big[\mu_{1}\partial_{Y|\Lambda}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}(\mu_{1}\partial_{x|\Lambda}^{2}+\omega^{2}\rho_{1})\big]\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle=\mu_{1}\partial_{Y|\Lambda}^{2}
and
(\mu_{1}\partial_{Y|\Gamma,Y=0^{-}}-\varepsilon\,\mu_{0}\,\partial_{y|\Gamma,y=0^{+}})\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle=\mu_{1}\partial_{Y|\Gamma,Y=0^{-}}
. Therefore
\mathbf{v}^{0}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\mu_{0}\Delta\mathbf{v}^{0}_{\varepsilon}&0&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
\mu_{1}\partial_{Y}^{2}\mathbf{v}^{0}_{\varepsilon}&0&\text{ in }\Lambda\\[1.42262pt]
\mu_{1}\partial_{Y|Y=0^{-}}\mathbf{v}^{0}_{\varepsilon}&0&\text{ on }\Gamma\end{array}\right.

Then Proposition
2.3
implies that there are numbers
c_{p,\ell,d}
s.t.
\mathbf{v}^{0}_{\varepsilon}=\fsum_{p,\ell,d}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,c_{p,\ell,d}\,\phi_{d}
. Finally we have
c_{p,\ell,d}=\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})
for any
(p,\ell,d)
because Proposition
2.3
gives that
\forall R\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon},\;\sigma_{d}\circ R=0
.

\scriptstyle\Box

Remark 2.27:

The elements of
\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
present in (
2.26
)–(
2.27
) are the
R_{1}\circ\cdots\circ R_{n}(\phi_{d})
with:
\forall i,\,R_{i}\in\{-k_{0}^{2}R_{\Delta},\,R_{\partial_{Y}^{2}}\circ\partial_{x|\Lambda}^{2},\,-k_{1}^{2}R_{\partial_{Y}^{2}},\,\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}R_{\mathrm{N}}\circ\partial_{y|\Gamma,y=0^{+}}\}
. They cannot be described using only inductive sequences and their number grows exponentially with
n
. That is why we introduced the operators
R_{\Delta}
,
R_{\partial_{Y}^{2}}
and
R_{\mathrm{N}}
. They allow a factorization of (
2.26
)–(
2.27
) into a geometric series of operators, that encapsulates all the complexity of the singularities, and the coefficents
\sigma_{d}(\dots)
, that need to be fixed for the different fields to match. This will allow us to reduce the matching condition to only fixing the coefficents
\sigma_{d}(\dots)
.

Now, we want to define
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}:\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{-}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)
as the scaling operator

{}\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi),\qquad\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}(X,Y)\mapsto\varphi(\varepsilon X,\varepsilon Y)&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
(X,Y)\mapsto\varphi(\varepsilon X,Y)&\text{ in }\Lambda\end{array}\right.

(2.30)

However,
\varepsilon
is an indeterminate. Let us first define
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Pi)
,
d\in\mathbb{R}
. If
\varphi_{|\Omega}
has the form
\Im[(\alpha z)^{q}\,\overline{\alpha z}^{k}P(\log(\alpha z))]
with
(q,k,P)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}[T]
s.t.
q+k=d
and
\mathcal{P}(q,k,P)
is true, we set:

{}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)_{|\Omega}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\deg P}\varepsilon^{d}\,\frac{\ln^{j}\!\varepsilon}{j!}\,\Im[(\alpha z)^{q}\,\overline{\alpha z}^{k}P^{(j)}(\log(\alpha z))]\in\varepsilon^{d}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega)[\ln\varepsilon].

(2.31)

And if
\varphi_{|\Lambda}
has the form
x^{d}\,Q(\ln(x),Y)
with
Q\in\mathbb{C}[T]
, we set:

{}\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)_{|\Lambda}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\deg Q}\varepsilon^{d}\,\frac{\ln^{j}\!\varepsilon}{j!}\,x^{d}\,\partial_{T}^{j}Q(\ln x,Y)\in\varepsilon^{d}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Lambda)[\ln\varepsilon].

(2.32)

Thanks to Lemmas
2.2
and
2.2
, it defines well
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
from
\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Pi)
to
\varepsilon^{d}\,\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Pi)[\ln\varepsilon]
. Then we extend
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
to
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)
by setting
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(\fsum_{p,d,\ell}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,\varphi_{p,d,\ell}):=\fsum_{p,d}\sum_{\ell}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi_{p,d,\ell})
for any
(\varphi_{p,d,\ell})
s.t.
\varphi_{p,d,\ell}\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Pi)
for any
(p,d,\ell)
. One can check that is in
\mathcal{A}^{-}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)
.

Remarks:

•

In practice, we will only use the informal definition of (
2.30
), but everything we will do can be checked using (
2.31
) and (
2.32
).

•

\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}
is invertible and
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
is roughly the scaling:
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(\varphi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l@{\,}l}(x,y)\mapsto\varphi(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{y}{\varepsilon})&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
(x,Y)\mapsto\varphi(\frac{x}{\varepsilon},Y)&\text{ in }\Lambda\end{array}\right.

•

For the first time, powers of
\ln\varepsilon
naturally appear because of the power of
\ln r
. This explains why the presence of these powers in the ansatz is necessary from the beginning.

Definition 2.28:

We rigorously rewrite the matching condition as:
\displaystyle\!\!\fsum_{p\in\mathbb{P},\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\cdot\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell}=\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\bigg(\fsum_{p\in\mathbb{P},\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\cdot S^{\infty}_{p,\ell}\bigg)
.

In order to rewrite the matching condition in terms of the coefficients
\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})
and
\sigma_{d}(S^{\infty}_{p,\ell})
, we define the following projectors of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)
:

\pi^{\pm}_{\sigma}\bigg(\fsum_{p\in\mathbb{P}}\fsum_{d\in\pm(\mathbb{P}-p)}\fsum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\cdot\varphi_{p,d,\ell}\bigg):=\fsum_{p\in\mathbb{P}}\fsum_{d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\cap\pm(\mathbb{P}-p)}\fsum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\cdot\sigma_{d}(\varphi_{p,d,\ell})\,\phi_{d}

for any
(\varphi_{p,d,\ell})
s.t.
\varphi_{p,d,\ell}\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Pi)
for any
(p,d,\ell)
.

Lemma 2.29:
The matching condition of Definition
2.5
is equivalent to each of these equalities:

\displaystyle\fsum_{p\in\mathbb{P},\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\!\!\!\fsum_{d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\cap(\mathbb{P}-p)}\!\!\!\!\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}_{p,\ell})\phi_{d}

\displaystyle=\pi^{+}_{\sigma}\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\bigg(\fsum_{p\in\mathbb{P},\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\!\!\!\fsum_{d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\cap(p-\mathbb{P})}\!\!\!\!\sigma_{d}(S^{\infty}_{p,\ell})\phi_{d}\bigg)

(2.33)

\displaystyle\fsum_{p\in\mathbb{P},\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\!\!\!\fsum_{d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\cap(p-\mathbb{P})}\!\!\!\!\sigma_{d}(S_{p,\ell})\phi_{d}

\displaystyle=\pi^{-}_{\sigma}\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\bigg(\fsum_{p\in\mathbb{P},\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\!\!\!\fsum_{d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\cap(\mathbb{P}-p)}\!\!\!\!\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})\phi_{d}\bigg)

(2.34)

.

Proof:
Let us prove only (
2.33
). By Theorem
2.5
, the matching condition of Definition
2.5
is equivalent to:

{}\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\bigg(\fsum_{p,\ell}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,\fsum_{d}\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})\phi_{d}\bigg)=\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\circ\bigg(\fsum_{p,\ell}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,\fsum_{d}\sigma_{d}(S^{\infty}_{p,\ell})\phi_{d}\bigg)

(2.35)

Moreover, Proposition
2.3
implies:
\forall R\in\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon},\;\pi^{+}_{\sigma}\circ R=0
. Since
\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle
is a sum of the identity and non-trivial products of elements of
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}
, we have
\pi^{+}_{\sigma}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle=\pi^{+}_{\sigma}\circ\mathrm{id}=\pi^{+}_{\sigma}
. Thus applying
\pi^{+}_{\sigma}
to (
2.35
) gives (
2.33
).

It remains to show conversely that (
2.33
) implies (
2.35
). Let us denote
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
the two sides of (
2.35
). One can check that they are solutions of (
2.29
). The proof of Theorem
2.5
shows that such solutions have the following form

A_{i}=\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\bigg(\fsum_{p,\ell,d}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,c_{i,p,\ell,d}\,\phi_{d}\bigg),\quad i\in\{1,2\}

for some numbers
c_{i,p,\ell,d}
. In addition, we have
\pi^{+}_{\sigma}(A_{i})=\fsum_{p,\ell,d}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,c_{i,p,\ell,d}\,\phi_{d}
, so
A_{i}
is entirely determined by
\pi^{+}_{\sigma}(A_{i})
. Since (
2.33
) states that
\pi^{+}_{\sigma}(A_{1})=\pi^{+}_{\sigma}(A_{2})
, we have
A_{1}=A_{2}
, i.e. (
2.35
).

\scriptstyle\Box

Remark:

Since
\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}
only differ on powers of
\varepsilon
due to the scaling, one could expect that
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle
is equal to
\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
. This would simplify a lot (
2.33
), which would become simply “
\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})=\sigma_{d}(S^{\infty}_{p+d,\ell})
” for any
(d,p,\ell)
. However,
R_{\Delta}
(resp.
R_{\partial_{Y}^{2}}
,
R_{\mathrm{N}}
) picks the particular solution of Equation
2.12
(resp.
2.13
,
2.14
) whose image by the
\sigma_{d}
vanish. This means that they are the solutions in the kernels of
\pi^{\pm}_{\sigma}
. It is easy to see that
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{\pm 1}
does not map
\operatorname{Ker}\pi^{\pm}_{\sigma}
to
\operatorname{Ker}\pi^{\mp}_{\sigma}
(e.g. consider an element of
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)
equal to
\Im[(\alpha z)^{\frac{\pi}{\Theta}}\log(\alpha z)]
on
\Omega
). Therefore
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ R_{\Delta}
selects other solutions than
R_{\Delta}\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
, and likewise for
R_{\partial_{Y}^{2}}
and
R_{\mathrm{N}}
, which yields
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\neq\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
. This observation explains the complexity of the matching relations in Theorem
2.5
.

For any
(p,\ell)
, let us define
\tau_{p,\ell}:\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Pi)
by:

\forall\varphi=\fsum_{p^{\prime}\!,\ell^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{p^{\prime}}\ln^{\ell^{\prime}}\!\varepsilon\,\varphi_{p^{\prime}\!,\ell^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi),\ \tau_{p,\ell}(\varphi):=\varphi_{p,\ell}
. It allows us to define the matching coefficients, that are for any
(d,d^{\prime},p,\ell)\in(\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{2}\times\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}
the following complex numbers:

{}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d^{\prime},p,\ell}:=\sigma_{d}\circ\tau_{p,\ell}\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle(\phi_{d^{\prime}})\quad\text{ and }\quad c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p,\ell}:=\sigma_{d}\circ\tau_{p,\ell}\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{+}_{\varepsilon}\rangle(\phi_{d^{\prime}})

(2.36)

In addition, for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote
[\![a,b]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}:=\{c\in\mathbb{R}\mid c-a\in\mathbb{P}\text{ and }b-c\in\mathbb{N}\}
. It is a finite subset of
[a,b]
.

Theorem
2.5
gives equations to concretely build the fields
\mathbf{u}_{p,\ell}
and
S_{p,\ell}
so that they match around the corner. It fixes their non-variational, which are determined by
\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})
when
d<0
and
\sigma_{d}(S^{\infty}_{p,\ell})
when
d>0
(see Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
). It also provides inductive formulas, depending on the fields with smaller
p
. Moreover these formulas have a convolutive structure w.r.t.
p
and
\ell
.

Theorem 2.30:
The matching condition of Definition
2.5
is equivalent to the following set of equations:

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall(d,p,\ell)\in(-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}){\times}\mathbb{P}{\times}\mathbb{N},\ \

\displaystyle\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})=\!\!\!\sum_{p^{\prime}\in[\![0,p+d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}}\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
p^{\prime}-d^{\prime}\in[\![0,p]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\!\!\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell}\,c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d^{\prime},p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(S^{\infty}_{p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}})

(2.37a)

\displaystyle\textstyle\forall(d,p,\ell)\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N},\ \

\displaystyle\sigma_{d}(S^{\infty}_{p,\ell})=\!\!\!\sum_{p^{\prime}\in[\![0,p-d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}}\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
p^{\prime}+d^{\prime}\in[\![0,p]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\!\!\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell}\,c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}})

(2.37b)

.

Proof:
Let
\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}
be the set of families of complex numbers
(\mathtt{u}_{p,d,\ell})_{p\in\mathbb{P},d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\cap(\mathbb{P}-p),\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
s.t. for any
(p,d)
only a finite number of the
\mathtt{u}_{p,d,\ell}
are non-zero. We define
\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}}
similarly by replacing “
d\in\mathbb{P}-p
” with “
d\in p-\mathbb{P}
”. Let
\sigma(\mathbf{u}):=(\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell}))_{p,d,\ell}\in\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}
and
\sigma(S):=(\sigma_{d}(S^{\infty}_{p,\ell}))_{p,d,\ell}\in\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}}
. We define the following linear maps:

\mathtt{P}:\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}}\rightarrow\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}},\ (\mathtt{S}_{p,d,\ell})_{p,d,\ell}\ \mapsto\ \bigg(\sum_{p^{\prime}\in[\![0,p+d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
p^{\prime}-d^{\prime}\in[\![0,p]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\!\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d^{\prime},p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\mathtt{S}_{p^{\prime},d^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}\bigg)_{\!p,d,\ell}\vskip-11.38109pt

\mathtt{Q}:\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}\rightarrow\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}},\ (\mathtt{u}_{p,d,\ell})_{p,d,\ell}\ \mapsto\ \bigg(\sum_{p^{\prime}\in[\![0,p-d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
p^{\prime}+d^{\prime}\in[\![0,p]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\!\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell}c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\mathtt{u}_{p^{\prime},d^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}\bigg)_{\!p,d,\ell}

These sums have a finite number of terms, so they are well-defined.

Step 1:
Let
(d,p,\ell)\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\times\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}
. Applying
\sigma_{d}\circ\tau_{p,\ell}
to the first equation of Lemma
2.5
gives:

\displaystyle\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P},\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}\ \sum_{d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\cap(\mathbb{P}-p^{\prime})}\sigma_{d}\circ\tau_{p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\circ\pi^{+}_{\sigma}\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle(\phi_{d^{\prime}})\cdot\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(S^{\infty}_{p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}}\ \sum_{d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\!,p^{\prime}+d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}}\ \sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d^{\prime},p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(S^{\infty}_{p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}})

because
\sigma_{d}\circ\tau_{p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\circ\pi^{+}_{\sigma}=\sigma_{d}\circ\tau_{p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}
. Moreover, for any
R\in\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}
, we have
\deg_{\varepsilon}\!R\in\mathbb{N}
and
\deg_{\varepsilon}\!R-\deg_{\mathcal{A}}\!R\in\mathbb{N}
(see Figure
9
). So for
c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d^{\prime},p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}
to be non-zero, we need
p-p^{\prime}+d\in\mathbb{N}
and
(p-p^{\prime}+d)-(d-d^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}
. In addition,
\ell-\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore, the matching condition in equivalent to
\sigma(\mathbf{u})=\mathtt{P}\,\sigma(S)
. Similarly, it is equivalent to
\sigma(S)=\mathtt{Q}\,\sigma(\mathbf{u})
.

Step 2:
We define the subspaces
\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}_{\pm}=\{\mathtt{u}\in\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}\mid\forall(p,d,\ell),\;\mathtt{u}_{p,d,\ell}\neq 0\mathrel{\mbox{$=\hskip-6.5pt\Rightarrow$}}\pm d>0\}
and likewise
\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}}_{\pm}
. So
\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}=\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}_{+}\oplus\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}_{-}
and similarly for
\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}}
. Let
\sigma(\mathbf{u})_{\pm}
be the components of
\sigma(\mathbf{u})
on
\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}_{\pm}
, and similarly for
\sigma(S)_{\pm}
. We decompose
\mathtt{P}
and
\mathtt{Q}
on those subspaces, which gives in block matrix notation:

\mathtt{P}\,=\,\raisebox{-9.47217pt}{$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}c}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.0pt}{17.22217pt}\mathtt{P}_{+}\\
\hline\cr\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.0pt}{17.22217pt}\mathtt{P}_{-}\end{array}\right)&\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\left.\rule{0.0pt}{9.47217pt}\right\}\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}_{+}\\[4.30554pt]
\left.\rule{0.0pt}{9.47217pt}\right\}\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}_{-}\end{array}\\[-8.1805pt]
\underbrace{\rule{23.68048pt}{0.0pt}}_{\textstyle\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}}}&\end{array}$}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathtt{Q}\,=\,\raisebox{-9.47217pt}{$\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}c@{}c}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.0pt}{17.22217pt}\mathtt{Q}_{+}\\
\hline\cr\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.0pt}{17.22217pt}\mathtt{Q}_{-}\end{array}\right)\;=&\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.0pt}{17.22217pt}\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{+}&\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{+}\\
\hline\cr\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.0pt}{17.22217pt}\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{-}&\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{-}\end{array}\right)&\begin{array}[]{@{}c@{}}\left.\rule{0.0pt}{9.47217pt}\right\}\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}}_{+}\\[4.30554pt]
\left.\rule{0.0pt}{9.47217pt}\right\}\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}}_{-}\end{array}\\[-8.1805pt]
&\underbrace{\rule{21.52771pt}{0.0pt}}_{\textstyle\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}_{+}}\underbrace{\rule{21.52771pt}{0.0pt}}_{\textstyle\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}_{-}}&\end{array}$}\vskip-2.84526pt

The present theorem rewrites as:

{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}l}\sigma(\mathbf{u})_{-}&\mathtt{P}_{-}\,\sigma(S)\\[1.42262pt]
\sigma(S)_{+}&\mathtt{Q}_{+}\,\sigma(\mathbf{u})\end{array}\right.

(2.38)

By Step 1, this is clearly a necessary condition for the matching. It remains to prove that it is sufficient. Let us show that (
2.38
) implies one of the two conditions of Step 1, e.g.
\sigma(\mathbf{u})=\mathtt{P}\,\sigma(S)
.

Note that by Step 1 we have for any
\mathtt{u}\in\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}
and
\mathtt{S}\in\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}}
:
\mathtt{u}=\mathtt{P}\mathtt{S}\mathrel{\mbox{$\Leftarrow\hskip-6.0pt\Rightarrow$}}\mathtt{S}=\mathtt{Q}\mathtt{u}
. So
\mathtt{P}
and
\mathtt{Q}
are inverses of each other. Hence
\mathtt{Q}(\sigma(\mathbf{u})-\mathtt{P}\,\sigma(S))=\mathtt{Q}\,\sigma(\mathbf{u})-\sigma(S)
. Projecting this onto
\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{S}}_{+}
we deduce:

\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{+}(\sigma(\mathbf{u})_{+}-\mathtt{P}_{+}\,\sigma(S))+\mathtt{Q}^{-}_{+}(\underbrace{\sigma(\mathbf{u})_{-}-\mathtt{P}_{-}\,\sigma(S)}_{=0})=\underbrace{\mathtt{Q}_{+}\,\sigma(\mathbf{u})-\sigma(S)_{+}}_{=0}.\vskip-14.22636pt

Let us show that
\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{+}
is injective.

Let
\mathtt{u}\in\mathtt{V}^{\mathtt{u}}_{+}\setminus\{0\}
and let us show that
\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{+}\mathtt{u}\neq 0
. Let
(p,d,\ell)
be the smallest triplet for lexicographic order such that
\mathtt{u}_{p,d,\ell}\neq 0
. The term of
\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{+}\mathtt{u}
of index
(p+d,d,\ell)
is :

\sum_{p^{\prime}\in[\![0,p]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\\
p^{\prime}+d^{\prime}\in[\![0,p+d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\!\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell}c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p+d-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\mathtt{u}_{p^{\prime},d^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}\,=\,c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d,d,0}\cdot\mathtt{u}_{p,d,\ell}.\vskip-2.84526pt

We claim that it is non-zero because
c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d,d,0}=1
. Indeed, in the sum

\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{(R_{1},\dots,R_{n})\in(\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon})^{n}}R_{1}\circ R_{2}\circ\dots\circ R_{n}

the only term of degree
(0,0)
w.r.t.
\mathcal{A}
and
\varepsilon
is the identity, which appears for
n=0
. So the component of
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle(\phi_{d})
in
\varepsilon^{d}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Pi)[\ln\varepsilon]
is
\varepsilon^{d}\phi_{d}
. Thus
c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d,d,0}=\sigma_{d}\circ\tau_{d,0}\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle(\phi_{d})=\sigma_{d}(\phi_{d})=1
. So
\mathtt{Q}^{+}_{+}\mathtt{u}\neq 0
.

We have proven that
\sigma(\mathbf{u})_{+}=\mathtt{P}_{+}\,\sigma(S)
. Given that
\sigma(\mathbf{u})_{-}=\mathtt{P}_{-}\,\sigma(S)
, we deduce
\sigma(\mathbf{u})=\mathtt{P}\,\sigma(S)
.

\scriptstyle\Box

Remarks 2.31:

•

Thanks to the tools of Section
2.3
, we can compute exactly and very quickly the coefficients
c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d^{\prime},p,\ell}
and
c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p,\ell}
. Moreover, these coefficients depend only on
\Theta
,
\omega
and
(\mu_{0},\mu_{1},\rho_{0},\rho_{1})
, but not on
\Omega_{1}
nor precisely on the functions
\mu
and
\rho
.

•

In the sums of Theorem
2.5
, the indexes
d
and
d^{\prime}
satisfy
d-d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}
. Indeed, on the one hand
d,d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}
. On the other we have in (
2.37a
) that
p^{\prime}\in[\![0,p+d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}
and
p^{\prime}-d^{\prime}\in[\![0,p]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}
, so
d-d^{\prime}=(p+d-p^{\prime})-(p-(p^{\prime}-d^{\prime}))\in\mathbb{N}-\mathbb{N}\subset\mathbb{Z}
(and likewise in (
2.37b
)). Note that the set
\mathbb{Z}\cap\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}
can be very small. If
\Theta\in\pi\mathbb{Q}
, then
\mathbb{Z}\cap\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}=b\mathbb{Z}
where
\Theta=\pi\frac{a}{b}
with
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\mathrm{gcd}(a,b)=1
. Otherwise,
\mathbb{Z}\cap\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}=\{0\}
.

3
Construction of the asymptotic expansion

Equations
1.3
–
1.5
and Theorem
2.5
give the equations that the fields
u_{p,\ell}
,
U_{p,\ell}
and
S_{p,\ell}
must satisfy. In this section, we will build these fields according to those conditions. First of all, let us express the layer fields with the far fields, so that only two types of fields remain to build. Let
(\mathcal{U}_{n})\in\mathbb{R}[Y]^{\mathbb{N}}
be the unique sequence of polynomials s.t. for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
:

{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime\prime}&0\\[1.42262pt]
\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime}(0)&\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}\\[1.42262pt]
\mathcal{U}_{0}(-1)&0\end{array}\right.\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\mathcal{U}_{n}^{\prime\prime}&-\mathcal{U}_{n-1}\\[1.42262pt]
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{\prime}(0)&0\\[1.42262pt]
\mathcal{U}_{n}(-1)&0\end{array}\right.

(3.1)

Lemma 3.1: expression of the layer fields

Let us assume that the fields
u_{p,\ell}
and
U_{p,\ell}
are regular enough (we will check later that they are). (
1.4
) implies for any
(p,\ell)\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}
and
(x,Y)\in\Lambda
:

{}U_{p,\ell}(x,Y)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\partial_{x}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})^{n}\partial_{y}u_{p-1-2n,\ell}(x,0)\cdot\mathcal{U}_{n}(Y).\vskip-2.84526pt

(3.2)

where this sum has a finite number of non-zero terms by the convention:
\forall p\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{P},\forall\ell\in\mathbb{N},\;u_{p,\ell}:=0
.

.

Proof:
There exists an increasing sequence
(p_{m})
s.t.
\mathbb{P}=\{p_{m}\mid m\in\mathbb{N}\}
. So we can prove the result by induction on
p\in\mathbb{P}
. For
p=0
, (
1.4
) states:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\partial_{Y}^{2}U_{0,\ell}&0&\text{ in }\Lambda\\[1.42262pt]
\partial_{Y}U_{0,\ell}&0&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[1.42262pt]
U_{0,\ell}&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Lambda}\end{array}\right.

so
U_{0,\ell}=0
for any
\ell
. It is coherent (
3.2
) (which is a sum of zeros in this case).

Next, for the inductive step, we assume that (
3.2
) holds for ranks smaller that
p
. (
1.4
) gives:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{}l@{}l@{\,}l}\partial_{Y}^{2}U_{p,\ell}&\;=\;-(\partial_{x}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})U_{p-2,\ell}&\;=\;-\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\partial_{x}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})^{n+1}\partial_{y}u_{(p-2)-1-2n,\ell}(x,0)\cdot\mathcal{U}_{n}(Y)&\\[1.42262pt]
&&\;=\;-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(\partial_{x}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})^{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}n}}\partial_{y}u_{p-1-2n,\ell}(x,0)\cdot\mathcal{U}_{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}n-1}}(Y)&\text{ in }\Lambda\\
\partial_{Y}U_{p,\ell}&\;=\;\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}\,\partial_{y}u_{p-1,\ell}&&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[1.42262pt]
U_{p,\ell}&\;=\;0&&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Lambda}\end{array}\right.

It is easy to see that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\partial_{x}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})^{n}\partial_{y}u_{p-1-2n,\ell}(x,0)\cdot\mathcal{U}_{n}(Y)
is the only solution of this.

\scriptstyle\Box

We saw in Section
2
that far and corner fields possess singularities when
r\rightarrow 0
, resp.
\mathbf{r}\rightarrow\infty
. So the usual variational frameworks are not sufficient to build these fields and we need to design new frameworks. It is done in Sections
3.1
and
3.2
. In both sections we start by introducing the natural space
H_{\mathrm{var}}
in which an ad hoc variational problem is well-posed. Then we define a bigger space
H:=H_{\mathrm{var}}+\chi\,\mathcal{A}(D)=\{u+\chi\varphi\mid u\in H_{\mathrm{var}},\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(D)\}
that contains the singularities, where
\chi
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
truncation function in the vicinity of 0 (for
u_{p,\ell}
) or infinity (for
S_{p,\ell}
), and
D\in\{\Omega,\Pi\}
. Next we determine the elements of
H_{\mathrm{var}}\cap\chi\mathcal{A}(D)
, which allows us to define on
H
the linear forms
\sigma_{d}
associated to the singularities. Finally we show that some model problems are well-posed in
H
.

Before we start, the following lemma is a tool to estimate the behavior at 0 and
\infty
of functions of
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 3.2:
Let
a<b
in
\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
(d_{i},\ell_{i})_{i\in[\![1,n]\!]}
be
n
distinct elements of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}
,
(f_{i})_{i\in[\![1,n]\!]}\in(\mathcal{C}([a,b],\mathbb{C})\setminus\{0\})^{n}
and:

\varphi:(r,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}\times[a,b]\;\mapsto\;\sum_{i=1}^{n}r^{d_{i}}\ln^{\ell_{i}}\!r\cdot f_{i}(\theta).

Then there is an interval
I\subset[a,b]
with non-empty interior,
c\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
and
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
such that:

\forall r\in(0,r_{1}),\forall\theta\in I,\ \ |\varphi(r,\theta)|>c\,r^{\min_{j}d_{j}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\forall r\in(r_{2},\infty),\forall\theta\in I,\ \ |\varphi(r,\theta)|>c\,r^{\max_{j}d_{j}}.

.

Proof:
Let
j\in[\![1,n]\!]
be s.t.
(d_{j},\ell_{j})
is maximal for the lexicographic order. Let
I\subset[a,b]
be a non trivial interval on which
|f_{j}|
is greater than a positive constant. Since
\sum_{i\neq j}r^{d_{i}}\ln^{\ell_{i}}\!r\cdot f_{i}(\theta)=o(r^{d_{j}}\ln^{\ell_{j}}r)
, we have when
r\rightarrow\infty
and
\theta\in I
:
|\varphi(r,\theta)|\gtrsim r^{d_{j}}\ln^{\ell_{j}}r\gtrsim r^{d_{j}}
. And we can similarly treat the vicinity of 0.

\scriptstyle\Box

We can apply Lemma
3
to any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega)
with
(a,b):=(0,\Theta)
, or to any
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Lambda)
with
(a,b):=(-1,0()
(replacing the variables
(r,\theta)
by
(x,Y)
). We can also apply it to
\partial_{r}\varphi
and
\partial_{\theta}\varphi
when
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega)
and to
\partial_{x}\varphi
and
\partial_{Y}\varphi
when
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Lambda)
.

Definition:

For any
D\in\{\Pi,\Omega,\Gamma,\Lambda\}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(D)
, we denote
\operatorname{deg_{min}}\varphi:=\sup\{d\in\mathbb{R}\mid\varphi\in\sum_{q\geqslant d}\mathcal{A}_{q}(D)\}
and
\operatorname{deg_{max}}\varphi:=\inf\{d\in\mathbb{R}\mid\varphi\in\sum_{q\leqslant d}\mathcal{A}_{q}(D)\}
.

3.1
Existence and uniqueness for far fields-like problems

We denote
H^{1/2}_{00}(\Gamma)
the set of functions of
H^{1/2}(\Gamma)
whose extension by 0 to
\partial\Omega=\Gamma\cup\{(0,0)\}\cup\Sigma_{\Omega}
is in
H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega)
. Using Lax-Milgram theorem, it is easy to prove the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3: the Helmholtz problem in
H^{1}(\Omega)

Let
f\in(H^{1}(\Omega))^{\prime}
and
g\in H^{1/2}_{00}(\Gamma)
. The following system has a unique solution in
H^{1}(\Omega)
.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\mu_{0}\Delta u+\omega^{2}\rho_{0}u&f&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
u&g&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[1.42262pt]
u&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Omega}\end{array}\right.

Let
\chi_{0}
be a radial function of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
equal to 1 in the vicinity of 0 and to 0 in the vicinity of infinity. The appropriate space to build the far fields is
H^{1}(\Omega)+\chi_{0}\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
. One can check that it does not depend on the choice of
\chi_{0}
.

Lemma 3.4:

\displaystyle H^{1}(\Omega)\cap\chi_{0}\mathcal{A}(\Omega)=\chi_{0}\sum_{d>0}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega).

.

Proof:
The inclusion
\supset
is easy to check, so we focus on
\subset
. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)\setminus\{0\}
be s.t.
\chi_{0}\varphi\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. There is
d\in\mathbb{R}
,
\varphi_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega)\setminus\{0\}
and
\varphi_{2}\in\sum_{q>d}\mathcal{A}_{q}(\Omega)
s.t.
\varphi=\varphi_{1}+\varphi_{2}
. If
\partial_{\theta}\varphi_{1}
were null everywhere, then would so too
\varphi_{1}
because
\varphi_{1|\Sigma_{\Omega}}=0
. But we assumed the contrary, so
\partial_{\theta}\varphi_{1}\neq 0
. Thus Lemma
3
implies that there is a non-trivial interval
I\subset[0,\Theta]
s.t.
|\partial_{\theta}\varphi_{1}(r,\theta)|\gtrsim r^{d}
when
r\rightarrow 0
and
\theta\in I
. Finally
r^{-1}\partial_{\theta}(\chi_{0}\varphi)\in L^{2}(\Omega)
implies that
d>0
.

\scriptstyle\Box

Définition 3.5:

For any
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)+\chi_{0}\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
and
d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we denote
\sigma_{d}(u):=\sigma_{d}(\varphi)
where
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
is s.t.
u-\chi_{0}\varphi\in H^{1}(\Omega)
(see Definition
2.3
for
\sigma_{d}(\varphi)
). It does not depend on the choice of
\varphi
thanks to Lemma
3.1
.

Theorem 3.6: existence and uniqueness for a far fields-like model problem

Let
f\in(H^{1}(\Omega))^{\prime}
,
g\in H^{1/2}_{00}(\Gamma)+\chi_{0}\mathcal{A}(\Gamma)
and
(s_{d})\in\mathbb{C}^{-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}
with finite support. The following system has a unique solution in
H^{1}(\Omega)+\chi_{0}\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\mu_{0}\Delta u+\omega^{2}\rho_{0}u&f&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
u&g&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[1.42262pt]
u&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Omega}\\[1.42262pt]
\sigma_{d}(u)&s_{d}&\ \forall d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.

.

Proof:
Let us show the existence first, and then the uniqueness.

Existence:
Let
\tilde{g}\in H^{1/2}_{00}(\Gamma)
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(\Gamma)
be s.t.
g=\tilde{g}+\chi_{0}\varphi
. We look for the solution in the form
u=\tilde{u}+\chi_{0}\psi
with
\tilde{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
and
\psi\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
. Let

\psi^{+}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-k_{0}^{2}R_{\Delta}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Omega$}})^{n}\bigg(R_{\mathrm{D}}^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Omega$}}(\varphi)+\sum_{d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}s_{d}\,\phi^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Omega$}}_{d}\bigg)\;\in\;\mathcal{A}^{+}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Omega)

(where
\phi^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Omega$}}_{d}:=\phi_{d|\Omega}
) and
\psi:=T_{\leqslant 2}(\psi^{+})
. Using Proposition
2.3
one can check by calculus the first system below (see also Lemma
2.4
for a similar result). Then, the second system below derives from
\psi\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
,
\psi=\psi^{+}-(\mathrm{id}-T_{\leqslant 2})(\psi^{+})
and
\deg\Delta=-2
(by Lemma
2.3
).

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\;}r@{\;=\;}l@{\;}l@{}}(\mu_{0}\Delta+\omega^{2}\rho_{0})\psi^{+}&0&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.13809pt]
\psi^{+}&\varphi&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[1.13809pt]
\psi^{+}&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Omega}\\[1.13809pt]
\sigma_{d}(\psi^{+})&s_{d}&\ \forall d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.\quad\mathrm{so}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\;}r@{}l@{}}(\mu_{0}\Delta+\omega^{2}\rho_{0})\psi&\;\in\,\mathcal{A}(\Omega)\cap\fsum\limits_{d>0}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega)=\sum\limits_{d>0}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega)\\[1.42262pt]
\psi_{|\Gamma}-\varphi&\;\in\,\mathcal{A}(\Gamma)\cap\fsum\limits_{d>2}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Gamma)=\sum\limits_{d>2}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Gamma)\\
\psi_{|\Sigma_{\Omega}}&\;=\;0\\[1.42262pt]
\sigma_{q}(\psi)&\;=\;s_{d}\;\ \forall d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.

Therefore
(\mu_{0}\Delta+\omega^{2}\rho_{0})(\chi_{0}\psi)\in L^{2}(\Omega)
and
\chi_{0}\,(\psi_{|\Gamma}-\varphi)\in H^{1/2}_{00}(\Gamma)
. Finally, thanks to Lemma
3.1
, we can take
\tilde{u}
as the unique solution in
H^{1}(\Omega)
of:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\mu_{0}\Delta\tilde{u}+\omega^{2}\rho_{0}\tilde{u}&f-(\mu_{0}\Delta+\omega^{2}\rho_{0})(\chi_{0}\psi)&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
\tilde{u}&\tilde{g}-\chi_{0}\,(\psi-\varphi)&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[1.42262pt]
\tilde{u}&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Omega}\end{array}\right.

Uniqueness:
Let
u
be a homogeneous solution. There is
\tilde{u}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
and
\psi\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
such that
u=\tilde{u}+\chi_{0}\psi
. Let
\varphi:=\Delta\psi+k_{0}^{2}\psi
. By Lemma
2.3
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
. Let us show that
\operatorname{deg_{min}}\varphi>-2
.

We denote
d:=\operatorname{deg_{min}}\varphi
and
\widetilde{\varphi}:=(\Delta+k_{0}^{2})(\chi_{0}\psi)
. By Lemma
3
, there is a non-trivial interval
I\subset[0,\Theta]
s.t. when
r\rightarrow 0
and
\theta\in I
:
\widetilde{\varphi}(r,\theta)=\varphi(r,\theta)\gtrsim r^{d}
. Besides,
\widetilde{\varphi}=-(\Delta+k_{0}^{2})\tilde{u}\,\in(H^{1}_{0}(\Omega))^{\prime}
. Let us test it with
\zeta_{q}:(r,\theta)\mapsto\widetilde{\varphi}(r,\theta)\cdot r^{q-2d-2}\,\big(1-\chi_{0}(2^{1/q}r)\big)\,\chi(\theta)
where
q>0
and
\chi\in\mathcal{D}(0,\Theta)\setminus\{0\}
is everywhere non negative. Since
\zeta_{q}\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)
:

\langle\widetilde{\varphi},\zeta_{q}\rangle:=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\Theta}\widetilde{\varphi}\cdot\zeta_{q}\cdot r\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}r\,\lesssim\,\|\zeta_{q}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega)}\qquad\forall q>0

Let us assume by contradiction that
d\leqslant-2
. It is not difficult to check that when
q\rightarrow 0^{+}
:

\langle\widetilde{\varphi},\zeta_{q}\rangle\gtrsim\int_{c_{1}2^{-\frac{1}{q}}}^{c_{2}}r^{d}\,r^{q-d-2}\,r\mathrm{d}r\gtrsim\frac{1}{q}\quad\text{ and }\quad\|\zeta_{q}\|_{H^{1}}\lesssim\|\nabla\zeta_{q}\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\bigg(\int_{c_{3}2^{-\frac{1}{q}}}^{c_{4}}r^{2(q-d-3)}\,r\mathrm{d}r\!\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\!\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}

with some constants
c_{i}
and Poincaré’s inequality. But it contradicts
\langle\widetilde{\varphi},\zeta_{q}\rangle\lesssim\|\zeta_{q}\|_{H^{1}}
. So
d>-2
.

Now let us show that
\operatorname{deg_{min}}\psi>0
.

Let us assume the contrary. Then there is
d\leqslant 0
,
\psi_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega)\setminus\{0\}
and
\psi_{2}\in\sum_{q>d}\mathcal{A}_{q}(\Omega)
s.t.
\psi=\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}
. Lemma
2.3
states that
\deg\Delta=-2
, so
\Delta\psi_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{d-2}(\Omega)
and
\Delta\psi_{2}\in\sum_{q>d-2}\mathcal{A}_{q}(\Omega)
. In addition,
\varphi\in\sum_{q>-2}\mathcal{A}_{q}(\Omega)
and
\mathcal{A}(\Omega)=\bigoplus_{q\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{A}_{q}(\Omega)
according to Lemma
2.2
. So identifying the coordinate of
\Delta\psi+k_{0}^{2}\psi=\varphi
in
\mathcal{A}_{d-2}(\Omega)
gives
\Delta\psi_{1}=0
.

Moreover,
\psi_{|\Gamma}=-\tilde{u}_{|\Gamma}\in H^{1/2}(\Gamma)
, so
\psi_{|\Gamma}\in\sum_{q>0}\mathcal{A}_{q}(\Gamma)
. Hence:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\Delta\psi_{1}&0&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
\psi_{1}&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Omega}\cup\Gamma\end{array}\right.

By Proposition
2.3
, it implies
d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\psi_{1}=\sigma_{d}(\psi_{1})\,\phi^{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Omega$}}_{d}
. However,
\sigma_{d}(\psi_{1})=\sigma_{d}(\psi)=\sigma_{d}(u)=0
, which contradicts
\psi_{1}\neq 0
.

Finally, Lemma
3.1
implies
\chi_{0}\psi\in H^{1}(\Omega)
, so
u\in H^{1}(\Omega)
. This means that
u=0
by Lemma
3.1
.

\scriptstyle\Box

3.2
Existence and uniqueness for corner fields-like problems

Definition 3.7: the variational space
V

We define
V:=\{v\in H^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1})\mid\nabla v\in L^{2}(\Omega_{1})\text{ and }v_{|\partial\Omega_{1}}=0\}
and the norm
\|v\|_{V}:=\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}
.

Here “
v_{|\partial\Omega_{1}}=0
” means that
\chi_{0}(\frac{\cdot}{R})\,v\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega_{1})
for any
R>0
, where
\chi_{0}(\frac{\cdot}{R}):(X,Y)\mapsto\chi_{0}\big(\frac{X}{R}\frac{,Y}{R}\big)
.

Lemma 3.7:

Any
v\in V
satisfies
\|v\|_{H^{1}(\Lambda\cap\{X>R_{\mathrm{c}}\})}\lesssim\|v\|_{V}
and
\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{v}{1+r}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}\lesssim\|v\|_{V}
.

.

Proof:
We denote
A:=\Lambda\cap\{X>R_{\mathrm{c}}\}
and
B:=B(0,R_{\mathrm{c}})
, and we recall that
R_{\mathrm{c}}>0
is s.t.
\Omega_{1}\setminus B=\Pi\setminus B
.

Poincaré’s inequality gives for a.e.
X>R_{\mathrm{c}}
:
\|v(X,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(-1,0)}\;\lesssim\;\|\partial_{Y}v(X,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(-1,0)}
. Integrating w.r.t.
X
the square of this then gives:
\|v\|_{L^{2}(A)}\lesssim\|\partial_{Y}v\|_{L^{2}(A)}\leqslant\|v\|_{V}
. So
\|v\|_{H^{1}(A)}\lesssim\|v\|_{V}
.

Next, a generalized Poincaré inequality on circular slices of
\Omega\setminus B
gives for a.e.
r>R_{\mathrm{c}}
:
\|v(r,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(0,\pi)}\lesssim\|\partial_{\theta}v(r,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(0,\pi)}+|v(r,\theta=0)|
. Then integrating w.r.t.
r
the square of this times
\frac{1}{1+r}
gives:

\int_{\Omega\setminus B}\frac{v^{2}}{(1+r)r}\,r\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}r\;\lesssim\;\int_{\Omega\setminus B}\frac{(\partial_{\theta}v)^{2}}{(1+r)r}\,r\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{\Gamma\setminus B}\frac{v^{2}}{1+r}\mathrm{d}r.

Therefore
\|\frac{v}{1+r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\setminus B)}^{2}\lesssim\|\nabla v\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\setminus B)}^{2}+\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\setminus B)}^{2}
. But we know that
\|v\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma\setminus B)}\lesssim\|v\|_{H^{1}(A)}\lesssim\|v\|_{V}
, so
\|\frac{v}{1+r}\|_{L^{2}(\Pi\setminus B)}\lesssim\|v\|_{V}
. Finally Poincaré’s inequality in
B
also gives
\|\frac{v}{1+r}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1}\cap B)}\lesssim\|v\|_{V}
.

\scriptstyle\Box

Lemma 3.8: the Poisson problem in
\Omega_{1}

Let
F:\Omega_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be s.t.
(1+r)F\in L^{2}(\Omega_{1})
, and
g\in L^{2}(\Gamma\cap\{X>R_{\mathrm{c}}\})
. The following system is well-posed in
V
.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\operatorname{div}(\mu\nabla S)&F&\text{ in }\Omega_{1}\setminus(\Gamma\cap\{X>R_{\mathrm{c}}\})\\[1.42262pt]
S&0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{1}\\[1.42262pt]
S_{|Y=0^{+}}-S_{|Y=0^{-}}&0&\text{ on }\Gamma\cap\{X>R_{\mathrm{c}}\}\\[1.42262pt]
\mu_{0}\partial_{Y}S_{|Y=0^{+}}-\mu_{1}\partial_{y}S_{|Y=0^{-}}&g&\text{ on }\Gamma\cap\{X>R_{\mathrm{c}}\}\end{array}\right.

.

Proof:
The variational formulation of this problem is:

\forall v\in V,\qquad\int_{\Omega_{1}}\mu\nabla S\cdot\nabla v=-\int_{\Omega_{1}}Fv+\int_{\Gamma\cap\{X>R_{\mathrm{c}}\}}gv.

The left-hand side is coercive by definition of
V
. Lemma
3.2
ensures that the right-hand side is continuous. Moreover it is easy to see that
V
is complete. So we can conclude using the Lax-Milgram theorem.

\scriptstyle\Box

Let
\chi_{\infty}
be a function of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
equal to 0 on
B(0,R_{\mathrm{c}})
and 1 in a vicinity of infinity. The proper space to build the corner fields is
V+\chi_{\infty}\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
. One can check that it does not depend on the choice of
\chi_{\infty}
.

Lemma 3.9:

V\cap\chi_{\infty}\mathcal{A}(\Pi)=\big\{\chi_{\infty}\varphi\;\big|\;\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(\Pi),\;\operatorname{deg_{max}}(\varphi_{|\Omega})<0\;\text{ and }\,\operatorname{deg_{max}}(\varphi_{|\Lambda})<-\frac{1}{2}\big\}.

.

Proof:
The inclusion
\supset
is easy to check, so we focus on
\subset
. Let
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
be s.t.
\chi_{\infty}\varphi\in V
. The same method as Lemma
3.1
shows that
\operatorname{deg_{max}}\varphi_{|\Omega}<0
. And using that
\partial_{Y}(\chi_{\infty}\varphi)\in L^{2}(\Lambda)
we likewise get
\operatorname{deg_{max}}(\varphi_{|\Lambda})<-\frac{1}{2}
.

\scriptstyle\Box

Definition 3.10:

For any
S\in V+\chi_{\infty}\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
and
d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we denote
\sigma_{d}(S):=\sigma_{d}(\varphi)
where
\varphi\in\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
is s.t.
S-\chi_{\infty}\varphi\in V
. It does not depend on the choice of
\varphi
thanks to Lemma
3.2
.

Theorem 3.11: existence and uniqueness for a corner fields-like model problem

Let
F:\Omega_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be s.t.
(1+r)F\in L^{2}(\Omega_{1})
,
\varphi:\Pi\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
be s.t.
\varphi_{|\Omega}\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
and
\varphi_{|\Lambda}\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda)
, and
(s_{d})\in\mathbb{C}^{\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}
with finite support. The following system has a unique solution in
V+\chi_{\infty}\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\operatorname{div}(\mu\nabla S)&F+\chi_{\infty}\varphi&\text{ in }\Omega_{1}\\[1.42262pt]
S&0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{1}\\[1.42262pt]
\sigma_{d}(S)&s_{d}&\ \forall d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.

Remark:

\varphi
is not just an element of
\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
because it may be discontinuous on
\Gamma
.

.

Proof:
This proof is similar to Theorem
3.1
. Let us show the existence first, and then the uniqueness.

Existence:
We look for the solution in the form
S=\tilde{S}+\chi_{\infty}\psi
with
\tilde{S}\in V
and
\psi\in\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
. More precisely we denote (using notation
\langle.\rangle
from Definition
2.1
)

\varphi^{-}:=\left\langle-R_{\partial_{Y}^{2}}\circ\partial_{X|\Lambda}^{2},\;\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}R_{\mathrm{N}}\circ\partial_{Y|\Gamma,Y=0^{+}}\right\rangle\bigg(\frac{1}{\mu_{0}}R_{\Delta}(\varphi_{|\Omega})+\frac{1}{\mu_{1}}R_{\partial_{Y}^{2}}(\varphi_{|\Lambda})+\sum_{d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}s_{d}\,\phi_{d}\bigg)\,\in\,\mathcal{A}^{-}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Pi)

and
\psi:=T_{\geqslant-2}(\varphi^{-})
. By Lemma
2.4
, we have the first system below. Then, similarly to the proof of Theorem
3.1
, one can check that it implies the second system below.

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll@{}}\mu_{0}\Delta\varphi^{-}&\varphi&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
\mu_{1}\Delta\varphi^{-}&\varphi&\text{ in }\Lambda\\[1.42262pt]
[\varphi^{-}]_{\Gamma}&0&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[1.42262pt]
[\mu\partial_{Y}\varphi^{-}]_{\Gamma}&0&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[1.42262pt]
\varphi^{-}&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Omega}\cup\Sigma_{\Lambda}\\[1.42262pt]
\sigma_{d}(\varphi^{-})&s_{d}&\ \forall d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.\quad\mathrm{so}\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\;}r@{}l@{}}(\mu_{0}\Delta\psi-\varphi)_{|\Omega}&\;\in\,\mathcal{A}(\Omega)\cap\fsum\limits_{d<-4}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega)=\sum\limits_{d<-4}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega)\\[1.42262pt]
(\mu_{1}\Delta\psi-\varphi)_{|\Lambda}&\;\in\,\mathcal{A}(\Lambda)\cap\fsum\limits_{d<-2}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Lambda)=\sum\limits_{d<-2}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Lambda)\\[-1.42262pt]
[\psi]_{\Gamma}&\;=\;0\\[1.42262pt]
[\mu\partial_{Y}\psi]_{\Gamma}&\;\in\,\mathcal{A}(\Gamma)\cap\fsum\limits_{d<-2}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Gamma)=\sum\limits_{d<-2}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Gamma)\\[-1.42262pt]
\psi_{|\Sigma_{\Omega}\cup\Sigma_{\Lambda}}&\;=\;0\\[1.42262pt]
\sigma_{q}(\psi)&\;=\;s_{d}\;\ \forall d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.

where
[{\dots}]_{\Gamma}
stands for the jump on
\Gamma
.
Therefore
(1+r)\,\big[\!\operatorname{div}\!\big(\mu\nabla(\chi_{\infty}\psi)\big)-\chi_{\infty}\varphi\big]\in L^{2}(\Omega_{1})
and
[\mu\partial_{Y}(\chi_{\infty}\psi)]_{\Gamma}\in L^{2}(\Gamma\cap\{X>R_{\mathrm{c}}\})
. Finally, thanks to Lemma
3.2
, we take
\tilde{S}
as the unique solution in
V
of:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\operatorname{div}(\mu\nabla\tilde{S})&F+\chi_{\infty}\varphi-\operatorname{div}\!\big(\mu\nabla(\chi_{\infty}\psi)\big)&\text{ in }\Omega_{1}\setminus(\Gamma\cap\{X>R_{\mathrm{c}}\})\\[1.42262pt]
\tilde{S}&0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{1}\\[1.42262pt]
[\mu\partial_{Y}\tilde{S}]_{\Gamma}&-[\mu\partial_{Y}(\chi_{\infty}\psi)]_{\Gamma}&\text{ on }\Gamma\cap\{X>R_{\mathrm{c}}\}\end{array}\right.

Uniqueness:
Let
S
be a homogeneous solution and let us show that
S=0
. There is
\tilde{S}\in V
and
\psi\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
s.t.
S=\tilde{S}+\chi_{\infty}\psi
. The proof decomposes into the following steps, all proven by contradiction.

1.

\operatorname{deg_{max}}(\Delta\psi_{|\Omega})<-2
:
Like in step 1 of the uniqueness proof of Theorem
3.1
, we test the inequality:

\int_{\Omega}\Delta(\chi_{\infty}\psi)\cdot\zeta=-\int_{\Omega}\Delta\tilde{S}\cdot\zeta=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\tilde{S}\cdot\nabla\zeta\lesssim\|\zeta\|_{V}\qquad\forall\zeta\in\mathcal{D}(\Omega)

with
\zeta_{q}:(r,\theta)\mapsto\Delta(\chi_{\infty}\psi)(r,\theta)\cdot r^{-q-2d-2}\,\chi_{0}(2^{-1/q}r)\,\chi(\theta)
when
q\rightarrow 0^{+}
, where
d:=\operatorname{deg_{max}}(\Delta\psi_{|\Omega})
and
\chi\in\mathcal{D}(0,\Theta)\setminus\{0\}
is everywhere non negative.

2.

\operatorname{deg_{max}}(\Delta\psi_{|\Lambda})<-\frac{1}{2}
:
This step works like the previous one.

3.

\operatorname{deg_{max}}[\mu\partial_{Y}\psi]_{\Gamma}<-\frac{1}{2}
:
Let
d:=\operatorname{deg_{max}}[\mu\partial_{Y}\psi]_{\Gamma}
and
\varphi:=[\mu\partial_{Y}(\chi_{\infty}\psi)]_{\Gamma}
. We have
\nabla\tilde{S}\in L^{2}(\Omega_{1})
and, by steps 1 and 2,
\operatorname{div}(\mu\nabla\tilde{S})=-\operatorname{div}\!\big(\mu\nabla(\chi_{\infty}\psi)\big)\in L^{2}(\Omega_{1})
. Thus
\varphi=-[\mu\partial_{Y}\tilde{S}]_{\Gamma}\in H^{-1/2}(\Gamma)
. Like previously, we test it with
\zeta_{q}:x\mapsto\varphi(x)\cdot x^{-q-2d-1}\,\chi_{0}(2^{-1/q}x)
when
q\rightarrow 0^{+}
. If
d\geqslant-\frac{1}{2}
, we get
\langle\varphi,\zeta_{q}\rangle_{\Gamma}\gtrsim\frac{1}{q}
and
\|\zeta_{q}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma)}\lesssim\|\zeta_{q}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma)}\lesssim\frac{1}{\sqrt{q}}
, which contradicts
\langle\varphi,\zeta_{q}\rangle_{\Gamma}\lesssim\|\zeta_{q}\|_{H^{1/2}(\Gamma)}
.

4.

\operatorname{deg_{max}}(\psi_{|\Lambda})<-\frac{1}{2}
:
Let
d:=\operatorname{deg_{max}}(\psi_{|\Lambda})
. There is
\psi_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Lambda)\setminus\{0\}
and
\psi_{2}\in\sum_{q<d}\mathcal{A}_{q}(\Lambda)
s.t.
\psi_{|\Lambda}=\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}
. By Lemma
2.3
,
\deg(\partial_{x|\Lambda}^{2})=-2
,
\deg(\partial_{Y|\Gamma,Y=0^{+}})=-1
and
\deg(\partial_{Y|\Lambda}^{2})=\deg(\partial_{Y|\Gamma,Y=0^{-}})=0
. In addition, Proposition
2.2
states that
\mathcal{A}(D)=\bigoplus_{q\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{A}_{q}(D)
for any
D\in\{\Lambda,\Gamma\}
. So by taking the coordinates of
\Delta\psi_{|\Lambda}
in
\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Lambda)
and of
[\mu\partial_{Y}\psi]_{\Gamma}
in
\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Gamma)
, we get if
d\geqslant-\frac{1}{2}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\partial_{Y}^{2}\psi_{1}&0&\text{ in }\Lambda\\[1.42262pt]
\partial_{Y|\Gamma,Y=0^{-}}\psi_{1}&0&\text{ on }\Gamma\end{array}\right.

But since
\psi_{|\Sigma_{\Lambda}}=0
, it implies
\psi_{1}=0
, which is contradictory.

5.

\operatorname{deg_{max}}(\psi_{|\Omega})<0
:
We denote
d:=\operatorname{deg_{max}}(\psi_{|\Omega})
. There is
\psi_{1}\in\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Omega)\setminus\{0\}
and
\psi_{2}\in\sum_{q<d}\mathcal{A}_{q}(\Omega)
s.t.
\psi_{|\Omega}=\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}
. Since
\deg(\Delta_{|\Omega})=-2
, taking the coordinate of
\Delta\psi_{|\Omega}
in
\mathcal{A}_{d-2}(\Omega)
and of
\psi_{|\Gamma}
in
\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Lambda)
gives if
d\geqslant 0
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\Delta\psi_{1}&0&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
\psi_{1}&0&\text{ on }\Gamma\end{array}\right.

By Proposition
2.3
, it implies
d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\psi_{1}=\sigma_{d}(\psi_{1})\phi_{q}
. But
\sigma_{d}(\psi_{1})=\sigma_{d}(\psi)=\sigma_{d}(S)=0
.

Finally, Lemma
3.2
implies
\chi_{\infty}\psi\in V
, so
S\in V
. So the uniqueness in Lemma
3.2
implies
S=0
.

\scriptstyle\Box

3.3
Construction of the fields

Definition 3.12:

For any
(p,\ell)\in(\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{P})\times\mathbb{N}
we denote by convention
u_{p,\ell}=0
,
U_{p,\ell}=0
and
S_{p,\ell}=0
. We define by induction on
p\in\mathbb{P}
that for any
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
:

•

\displaystyle U_{p,\ell}:(x,Y)\in\Lambda\mapsto\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\partial_{x}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})^{n}\partial_{y}u_{p-1-2n,\ell}(x,0)\cdot\mathcal{U}_{n}(Y)
. (3.3)

•

u_{p,\ell}
is the unique solution in
H^{1}(\Omega)+\chi_{0}\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
of :

{}\hskip-14.22636pt\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll@{}}\mu_{0}\Delta u_{p,\ell}+\omega^{2}\rho_{0}u_{p,\ell}&f_{\mathrm{s}}\,\delta_{p,0}\,\delta_{\ell,0}&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
u_{p,\ell}&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Omega}\\[1.42262pt]
u_{p,\ell}&U_{p,\ell}&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[-5.69054pt]
\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell})&\displaystyle\!\!\sum_{p^{\prime}\in[\![0,p+d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
p^{\prime}-d^{\prime}\in[\![0,p]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\!\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell}\,c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d^{\prime},p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(S^{\infty}_{p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}})&\ \forall d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.\vskip-5.69054pt

(3.4)

•

S_{p,\ell}
is the unique solution in
V+\chi_{\infty}\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
of :

{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\operatorname{div}(\mu\nabla S_{p,\ell})&-\omega^{2}\rho\,S_{p-2,\ell}&\text{ in }\Omega_{1}\\[1.42262pt]
S_{p,\ell}&0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{1}\\[-5.69054pt]
\sigma_{d}(S_{p,\ell})&\displaystyle\!\!\sum_{p^{\prime}\in[\![0,p-d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
p^{\prime}+d^{\prime}\in[\![0,p]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\!\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell}\,c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}})&\ \forall d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.

(3.5)

We will show that these fields are well-defined at the same time as the following proposition.

Proposition 3.13:

For any
(p,\ell)\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}
there exist
\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell}\in\mathcal{A}^{+}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Pi)
and
S^{\infty}_{p,\ell}\in\mathcal{A}^{-}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Pi)
s.t., for any
d\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathbf{u}_{p,\ell}=T_{\leqslant d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})+o_{\partial}(\mathbf{r}^{d})
when
\mathbf{r}\rightarrow 0
and
S_{p,\ell}=T_{\geqslant d}(S^{\infty}_{p,\ell})+o_{\partial}(\mathbf{r}^{d})
when
\mathbf{r}\rightarrow\infty
.

.

Justification of Definition
3.3
and Proposition
3.3
:

Let us show by induction on
p
that for any
\ell
:

(H1)

u_{p,\ell}
,
U_{p,\ell}
and
S_{p,\ell}
exist and are unique,

(H2)

Proposition
3.3
is true at rank
(p,\ell)
,

(H3)

\forall m\in\mathbb{N},\;\partial_{x}^{m}\partial_{y}u_{p,\ell|\Gamma}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma)+\chi_{0}\mathcal{A}(\Gamma)
(additional property that will be useful during the proof).

Since all fields are null for
p<0
, the initial case is trivial and only the inductive step remains to prove. Let
p\in\mathbb{P}
. Let us assume (H1)–(H3) at any rank
p^{\prime}<p
and prove it at rank
p
.

1.

Existence and uniqueness of the fields:

•

U_{p,\ell}
:
By (H3), the
\partial_{y}u_{p^{\prime},\ell|\Gamma}
with
p^{\prime}<p
are all in
H^{m}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Gamma)
for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
. So the functions
(\partial_{x}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})^{n}\partial_{y}u_{p-1-2n,\ell|\Gamma}
are continuous and Formula (
•
‣
3.3
) is well-defined at any point of
\Lambda
.

•

u_{p,\ell}
:
By (H2),
S^{\infty}_{p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}}
exists for any
p^{\prime}<p
and
\ell^{\prime}
, so the coefficients
\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(S^{\infty}_{p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}})
are well-defined. Thus, (
3.4
) defines well
\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell})
. And we have:
\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell})\neq 0\mathrel{\mbox{$=\hskip-6.5pt\Rightarrow$}}[\![0,p+d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}\neq\varnothing\mathrel{\mbox{$=\hskip-6.5pt\Rightarrow$}}d\geqslant-p
, so
(\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell}))_{d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}
has finite support. Next, we apply Theorem
3.1
. Its hypotheses are satisfied, because (H3) implies:

U_{p,\ell|\Gamma}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\partial_{x}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})^{n}\partial_{y}u_{p-1-2n,\ell}(x,0)\cdot\mathcal{U}_{n}(0)\,\in\,H^{1}_{0}(\Gamma)+\chi_{0}\mathcal{A}(\Gamma)\,\subset\,H^{1/2}_{00}(\Gamma)+\chi_{0}\mathcal{A}(\Gamma)\vskip-2.84526pt

•

S_{p,\ell}
:
Similarly, (H2) implies that
(\sigma_{d}(S_{p,\ell}))_{d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}
is well defined and has finite support. Next, we apply Theorem
2.4
. We need to check its hypotheses, i.e. there is
f:\Omega_{1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
and
\varphi:\Pi\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
s.t.
\omega^{2}\rho\,S_{p-2,\ell}=F+\chi_{\infty}\varphi
,
(1+r)F\in L^{2}(\Omega_{1})
,
\varphi_{|\Omega}\in\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
and
\varphi_{|\Lambda}\in\mathcal{A}(\Lambda)
. By (H2), there is
\psi\in\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
s.t.
S_{p-2,\ell}=\psi+o_{\partial}(\mathbf{r}^{-4})
. So it suffices to set
\varphi:=\omega^{2}\rho\psi
and
F:=\omega^{2}\rho\,S_{p-2,\ell}-\chi_{\infty}\varphi
.

2.

Asymptotic expansions:

•

\mathbf{u}_{p,\ell}
:
We use Theorem
2.4
. To do so, we must check that there is
g_{\Gamma}\in\mathcal{A}^{+}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Gamma)
and
g_{\Lambda}\in\mathcal{A}^{+}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Lambda)
s.t.:

\forall d\in\mathbb{R},\qquad\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\partial_{Y}^{2}U_{p,\ell}&T_{\leqslant d}(g_{\Lambda})+o_{\partial}(x^{d})&\text{ in }\Lambda\\[1.42262pt]
\partial_{Y}U_{p,\ell|Y=0^{-}}&T_{\leqslant d}(g_{\Gamma})+o_{\partial}(x^{d})&\text{ on }\Gamma\end{array}\right.

Given the definition of
U_{p,\ell}
(
•
‣
3.3
) , it suffices to show that, for any
p^{\prime}<p
there is
h\in\mathcal{A}^{+}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Gamma)
s.t.:
\forall d\in\mathbb{R},\;\partial_{n}u_{p^{\prime}\!,\ell|\Gamma}=T_{\leqslant d}(h)+o_{\partial}(x^{d})
. But it derives from (H2).

•

S_{p,\ell}
:
Similarly, we use Theorem
2.4
thanks to (H2).

3.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
. The asymptotic expansion of
\mathbf{u}_{p,\ell}
implies:
\exists h\in\mathcal{A}(\Gamma),\;\partial_{x}^{m}\partial_{y}u_{p,\ell|\Gamma}=h+o_{\partial}(x^{1})
. So there is
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
s.t.
\partial_{x}^{m}\partial_{y}u_{p,\ell|\Gamma}-h
is
H^{1}
on
\Gamma\cap\{x<x_{0}\}=(0,x_{0})\times\{0\}
and it vanishes at 0. To prove (H3) at rank
p
, it remains to show that
\partial_{x}^{m}\partial_{y}u_{p,\ell}\in H^{1}((\frac{x_{0}}{2},\infty)\times\{0\})
. To do so, it suffices to get
u\in H^{m+3}((\frac{x_{0}}{2},\infty)\times(0,\frac{\delta}{2}))
with
\delta:=\operatorname{dist}(\operatorname{supp}(f),\Gamma)
. But it follows from classical elliptic regularity because on one side
\mu_{0}\Delta u_{p,\ell}+\omega^{2}\rho_{0}u_{p,\ell}=0
on
(\frac{x_{0}}{4},\infty)\times(0,\delta)
, and on the other
u_{p,\ell|\Gamma}=U_{p,\ell|\Gamma}\in H^{m+3}((\frac{x_{0}}{4},\infty)\times\{0\})
by (
•
‣
3.3
) and (H3).

\scriptstyle\Box

Proposition 3.14:

\forall p\in\mathbb{P},\exists n_{p}\in\mathbb{N},\forall\ell>n_{p},\ \ (u_{p,\ell}=0\text{ and }U_{p,\ell}=0\text{ and }S_{p,\ell}=0)
.

.

Proof:
For any
(d,d^{\prime},p)\in(\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*})^{2}\times\mathbb{P}
, one has
\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle(\phi_{d^{\prime}})\in\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)
, so by definition of
\mathcal{A}^{+}_{\varepsilon}(\Pi)
there is
n
(depending of
d,d^{\prime},p
) s.t. for any
\ell>n
:
c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d^{\prime},p,\ell}:=\sigma_{d}\circ\tau_{p,\ell}\circ\mathcal{H}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\circ\langle\mathcal{R}^{-}_{\varepsilon}\rangle(\phi_{d^{\prime}})=0
. The same is true for the coefficients
c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p,\ell}
. Finally the result follows by induction from Definition
3.3
.

\scriptstyle\Box

Proposition 3.15:

Ansatz
2.5
and the matching condition of Definition
2.5
are satisfied.

.

Proof:
First let us note that for any
(p,\ell)

{}\textstyle\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell})=\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})\text{ when }d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\quad\text{ and }\quad\sigma_{d}(S_{p,\ell})=\sigma_{d}(S^{\infty}_{p,\ell})\text{ when }d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}.

(3.6)

where
\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell})
, resp.
\sigma_{d}(S_{p,\ell})
, is set by Definition
3.1
, resp.
3.2
, whereas
\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})
and
\sigma_{d}(S^{\infty}_{p,\ell})
rest on the definition of
\sigma_{d}
on
\mathcal{A}^{\pm}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Pi)
at page
2.4
. Indeed we have
u_{p,\ell}-T_{\leqslant 1}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})_{|\Omega}=o_{\partial}(r^{1})
, so
\chi_{0}\cdot(u_{p,\ell}-T_{\leqslant 1}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})_{|\Omega})\in H^{1}(\Omega)
which implies by Definition
3.1
that
\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell})=\sigma_{d}\big(T_{\leqslant 1}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})_{|\Omega}\big)=\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})
. Likewise for
S_{p,\ell}
.

Now, given Proposition
3.3
, to prove Ansatz
2.5
it suffices to check that, for any
(p,\ell)
,
\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell}\in\fsum_{d\in\mathbb{P}-p}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Pi)
and
S^{\infty}_{p,\ell}\in\fsum_{d\in p-\mathbb{P}}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Pi)
. Let us show it for
\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell}
only, using induction.

Since all fields vanish for
p<0
, only the inductive step is non-trivial. (
2.25
) states that

\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-k_{0}^{2}\,R_{\Delta})^{n}\bigg(\underbrace{R_{\partial_{Y}^{2}}\circ(\partial_{x|\Lambda}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p-2,\ell})+{\textstyle\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}}R_{\mathrm{N}}\circ\partial_{y|\Gamma,y=0^{+}}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p-1,\ell})}_{:=A}+\!\!\fsum_{d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\!\!\!\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})\,\phi_{d}\bigg).\vskip-2.84526pt

Since
\deg R_{\Delta}\in\mathbb{N}
, it suffices to show that the big brackets belong to
\fsum_{d\in\mathbb{P}-p}\mathcal{A}_{d}(\Pi)
. This is true for
A
using the induction hypothesis and
\deg R_{\partial_{Y}^{2}}=0
,
\deg(\partial_{x|\Lambda}^{2})=-2
and
\deg(R_{\mathrm{N}}\circ\partial_{y|\Gamma,y=0^{+}})=-1
(see Proposition
2.3
and Lemma
2.3
). It remains to show that
\forall d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\!,\;\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})\neq 0\mathrel{\mbox{$=\hskip-6.5pt\Rightarrow$}}d\in\mathbb{P}-p
. This last assertion holds because on one hand
\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\subset\mathbb{P}-p
, and on the other (
3.6
) and (
3.4
) imply:
\forall d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*},\;\sigma_{d}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})\neq 0\mathrel{\mbox{$\Leftarrow\hskip-6.0pt\Rightarrow$}}\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell})\neq 0\mathrel{\mbox{$=\hskip-6.5pt\Rightarrow$}}[\![0,p+d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}\neq\varnothing\mathrel{\mbox{$=\hskip-6.5pt\Rightarrow$}}d\in\mathbb{P}-p
.

Finally (
3.4
)–(
3.5
) and (
3.6
) show that the matching relations of Theorem
2.5
are satisfied, and we can apply Theorem
2.5
thanks to Ansatz
2.5
. Thus the matching condition of Definition
2.5
is satisfied.

\scriptstyle\Box

3.4
Practical way to build the far fields

This section shows how to build directly the far fields without computing the layer and corner fields. Thanks to the explicit expression of the layer fields in (
•
‣
3.3
), the layer is replaced by boundary conditions on
\Gamma
, while the corner fields are replaced by corner conditions depending on corner profiles.

Definition 3.16: corner profiles

Let
d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}
. We denote
(\mathcal{S}_{d,n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the unique sequence of
V+\chi_{\infty}\mathcal{A}(\Pi)
s.t. for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}l@{\;}l}\operatorname{div}(\mu\nabla\mathcal{S}_{d,0})&0&\text{ in }\Omega_{1}\\[1.42262pt]
\mathcal{S}_{d,0}&0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{1}\\[1.42262pt]
\sigma_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{d,0})&\delta_{d,q}&\ \forall q\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.\quad\text{ and }\quad\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}l@{\;}l}\operatorname{div}(\mu\nabla\mathcal{S}_{d,n})&-\omega^{2}\rho\,\mathcal{S}_{d,n-1}&\text{ in }\Omega_{1}\\[1.42262pt]
\mathcal{S}_{d,n}&0&\text{ on }\partial\Omega_{1}\\[1.42262pt]
\sigma_{q}(\mathcal{S}_{d,n})&0&\ \forall q\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.

And for any
(d,n)
we denote
\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{d,n}
the element of
\mathcal{A}^{-}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Pi)
s.t.
\forall d\in\mathbb{R},\;\mathcal{S}_{d,n}=T_{\geqslant d}(\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{d,n})+o_{\partial}(\mathbf{r}^{d})
.

These objects are well-defined thanks to Theorems
3.2
and
2.4
. The proof is the same as for Definition
3.3
and Proposition
3.3
.

Using (
3.5
) and the uniqueness in Theorem
3.2
, one can easily show by induction on
p
:

{}\forall(p,\ell)\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N},\quad S_{p,\ell}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\;\sum_{d\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\cap(p-2n-\mathbb{P})}\sigma_{d}(S_{p-2n,\ell})\,\mathcal{S}_{d,n}

(3.7)

Thus, the same holds replacing
S_{p,\ell}
and
\mathcal{S}_{d,n}
by resp.
S^{\infty}_{p,\ell}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{d,n}
.

For any
(d,d^{\prime},p,\ell)\in(-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*})\times\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\times\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}
, we introduce the corner coefficient
c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime}\!,p,\ell}
, we have

\displaystyle c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime}\!,p,\ell}

\displaystyle:=\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(p_{1},p_{2})\in(\mathbb{N}-d)\times(\mathbb{N}+d^{\prime})\\
n\in\mathbb{N},\,p_{1}+p_{2}+2n=p\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{1}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\\
p_{1}+d_{1}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{2}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\\
p_{2}-d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\\
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell\end{subarray}}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d_{1},p_{1},\ell_{1}}\cdot\sigma_{d_{1}}(\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{d_{2},n})\cdot c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d_{2},d^{\prime}\!,p_{2},\ell_{2}}

(3.8a)

\displaystyle\,=\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(p_{1},p_{2})\in(\mathbb{N}-d)\times(\mathbb{N}+d^{\prime})\\
n\in\mathbb{N},\,p_{1}+p_{2}+2n=p\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{1}\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}\\
p_{1}+d_{1}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d_{2}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\\
p_{2}-d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\\
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell\end{subarray}}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d_{1},p_{1},\ell_{1}}\cdot\sigma_{d_{1}}(\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{d_{2},n})\cdot c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d_{2},d^{\prime}\!,p_{2},\ell_{2}}

(3.8b)

\displaystyle\quad+\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(p_{1},p_{2})\in(\mathbb{N}-d)\times(\mathbb{N}+d^{\prime})\\
p_{1}+p_{2}=p\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}d_{1}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}\\
p_{1}+d_{1}\in\mathbb{N},\,p_{2}-d_{1}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\ \sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\\
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell\end{subarray}}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d_{1},p_{1},\ell_{1}}\cdot c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d_{1},d^{\prime}\!,p_{2},\ell_{2}}

(3.8c)

(the two given formulas are equal because, when
d_{1}>0
,
\sigma_{d_{1}}(\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{d_{2},n})=\sigma_{d_{1}}(\mathcal{S}_{d_{2},n})=\delta_{d_{1},d_{2}}\,\delta_{n,0}
). Like in Remark
2.5
, we have
d-d_{1},d_{2}-d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}\cap\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}
in (
3.8a
). Thus, if
d-d^{\prime}\not\in\mathbb{Z}\cap\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}
, line (
3.8c
) vanishes. Moreover, for any
(p,\ell)
, we denote
u^{0}_{p,\ell}:=\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell|\Omega}\in\mathcal{A}^{+}\hskip-0.6458pt(\Omega)
, which satisfies:
\forall d\in\mathbb{R},\;u_{p,\ell}\underset{r\rightarrow 0}{=}T_{\leqslant d}(u^{0}_{p,\ell})+o_{\partial}(r^{d})
.

Theorem 3.17: direct construction of the far fields

Let
(T_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be the sequence of Taylor coefficients of the tangent:
\forall t\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}),\;\tan t=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}T_{n}t^{2n+1}
.

(u_{p,\ell})_{p\in\mathbb{P},\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
is the unique family of
H^{1}(\Omega)+\chi_{0}\mathcal{A}(\Omega)
s.t. for any
(p,\ell)\in\mathbb{P}\times\mathbb{N}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll@{}}\mu_{0}\Delta u_{p,\ell}+\omega^{2}\rho_{0}u_{p,\ell}&f_{\mathrm{s}}\,\delta_{p,0}\,\delta_{\ell,0}&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
u_{p,\ell}&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Omega}\\[1.42262pt]
u_{p,\ell}&\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}\,T_{n}\cdot(\partial_{x}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})^{n}\partial_{y}u_{p-1-2n,\ell}&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[-4.2679pt]
\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell})&\displaystyle\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}\\
p-p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}+\frac{2\pi}{\Theta}\end{subarray}}\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}\\
p^{\prime}+d^{\prime}\in[\![0,p+d]\!]_{\mathbb{P}\!,\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\!\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p-p^{\prime},\ell-\ell^{\prime}}\cdot\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(u^{0}_{p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}})&\ \forall d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.

.

Proof:
Uniqueness follows from the uniqueness in Theorem
3.1
. So it suffices to prove that the far fields satisfy the equations above.

Boundary condition:
By (
•
‣
3.3
) and (
3.4
), we have

u_{p,\ell}(x,0)=U_{p,\ell}(x,0)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(\partial_{x}^{2}+k_{1}^{2})^{n}\partial_{y}u_{p-1-2n,\ell}(x,0)\cdot\mathcal{U}_{n}(0).

So we need to calculate
\mathcal{U}_{n}(0)
. Let
\mathrm{U}:(Y,t)\mapsto\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{n}(Y)\,t^{2n+1}
. The definition of
(\mathcal{U}_{n})
(reminded in (
3.9
) below) formally implies a differential equation on
\mathrm{U}
given in (
3.10
).

{}\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\mathcal{U}_{n}^{\prime\prime}&-\mathcal{U}_{n-1}\\[1.42262pt]
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{\prime}(0)&\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}\delta_{n,0}\\[1.42262pt]
\mathcal{U}_{n}(-1)&0\end{array}\right.

(3.9)

{}\left\{\begin{array}[]{r@{\;=\;}ll}\partial_{Y}^{2}\text{U}&-t^{2}\text{U}\\[1.42262pt]
\partial_{Y}\text{U}_{|Y=0}&\mathcal{U}_{0}^{\prime}(0)\cdot t=\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}t\\[1.42262pt]
\text{U}_{|Y=-1}&0\end{array}\right.

(3.10)

Thus
\mathrm{U}(Y,t)=\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}\frac{\sin(t(Y+1))}{\cos(t)}:=\varphi(Y,t)
. This is formal, as we do not know whether the series
\mathrm{U}
converges. However, there is a sequence of polynomial functions
(\Phi_{n})
s.t., for any
(Y,t)\in(-1,0)\times(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2})
,
\varphi(Y,t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Phi_{n}(Y)\,t^{2n+1}
(because
\varphi
is odd w.r.t.
t
). Since
\varphi
satisfies (
3.10
),
(\Phi_{n})
is a solution of (
3.9
). But this solution is unique, so
(\Phi_{n})=(\mathcal{U}_{n})
and
\mathrm{U}=\varphi
. Hence
\forall t\in(-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}),\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{U}_{n}(0)\,t^{2n+1}=\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}\tan(t)
. That is to say
\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\;\mathcal{U}_{n}(0)=\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}\,T_{n}
.

Corner condition:
For any
(d,p,\ell)\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\infty}_{d,p,\ell}:=\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{d,p/2}
if
\frac{p}{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\ell=0
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\infty}_{d,p,\ell}:=0
otherwise. For any family
(x_{p,\ell})
, we denote
x_{\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}}:=(x_{p,\ell})
the family itself. We also denote
*
the convolution product w.r.t.
(p,\ell)
. Let
d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}
. We have

\displaystyle\sigma_{d}(u_{\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{d_{1}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d_{1},\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}}*\sigma_{d_{1}}(S^{\infty}_{\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}})

by (
3.4
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{d_{1}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d_{1},\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}}*\sum_{d_{2}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}\sigma_{d_{1}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\infty}_{d_{2},\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}})*\sigma_{d_{2}}(S_{\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}})

by (
3.7
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{d_{1}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d_{1},\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}}*\sum_{d_{2}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}\sigma_{d_{1}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\infty}_{d_{2},\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}})*\sum_{d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}}c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d_{2},d^{\prime}\!,\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}}*\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}})

by (
3.5
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\bigg(\sum_{d_{1}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{d_{2}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d_{1},\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}}*\sigma_{d_{1}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}_{d_{2},\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}})*c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d_{2},d^{\prime}\!,\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}}\bigg)*\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(u^{0}_{\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{d^{\prime}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\tilde{c}^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime}\!,\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}}*\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(u^{0}_{\text{\tiny$\bullet$},\text{\tiny$\bullet$}})

with
\displaystyle\tilde{c}^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p,\ell}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(p_{1},p_{2}),n\in\mathbb{N}\\
p_{1}+p_{2}+2n=p\end{subarray}}\sum_{d_{1}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{Z}^{*}}\sum_{d_{2}\in\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\\
\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell\end{subarray}}c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d_{1},p_{1},\ell_{1}}\cdot\sigma_{d_{1}}(\mathcal{S}^{\infty}_{d_{2},n})\cdot c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d_{2},d^{\prime}\!,p_{2},\ell_{2}}
.

Moreover, for any
R\in\mathcal{R}^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}
, we have
\deg_{\varepsilon}\!R\in\mathbb{N}
and
\deg_{\varepsilon}\!R\pm\deg_{\mathcal{A}}\!R\in\mathbb{N}
(see Figure
9
). So by definition of the coefficients
c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{\dots}
and
c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{\dots}
:

•

c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow S}_{d,d_{1},p_{1},\ell_{1}}\neq 0\mathrel{\mbox{$=\hskip-6.5pt\Rightarrow$}}p_{1}+d\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }p_{1}+d_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

c^{S\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d_{2},d^{\prime},p_{2},\ell_{2}}\neq 0\mathrel{\mbox{$=\hskip-6.5pt\Rightarrow$}}p_{2}-d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }p_{2}-d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.

This implies that
\tilde{c}^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p,\ell}=c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p,\ell}
for any
(d,d^{\prime},p,\ell)
.

Furthermore, those conditions on
(p_{1,}p_{2},d_{1},d_{2})
imply that, if
c^{\mathbf{u}\leftarrow\mathbf{u}}_{d,d^{\prime},p,\ell}\neq 0
, then:

•

p=(p_{1}+d)+2n+(p_{2}-d_{2})-d+d_{2}\in\mathbb{N}+\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}+\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\subset\mathbb{P}+\frac{2\pi}{\Theta}

•

and
p+d-d^{\prime}=(p_{1}+d)+2n+(p_{2}-d^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}
.

This and the property
(\sigma_{d^{\prime}}(u_{p^{\prime},\ell^{\prime}})\neq 0\mathrel{\mbox{$=\hskip-6.5pt\Rightarrow$}}p^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P}\text{ and }p^{\prime}+d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{P})
explain the sum indexes of the formula given for
\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell})
in Theorem
3.4
.

\scriptstyle\Box

Example:

Using Theorem
3.4
, one can check that
u_{\pi/\Theta,\ell}
vanishes for any
\ell
. In addition
\frac{2\pi}{\Theta}>1
, so the first non-zero far fields are
u_{0,0}
and
u_{1,0}
and they satisfy

\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\;}r@{\;=\;}l@{\;\;}l@{}}\mu_{0}\Delta u_{0,0}+\omega^{2}\rho_{0}u_{0,0}&f_{\mathrm{s}}&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
u_{0,0}&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Omega}\\[1.42262pt]
u_{0,0}&0&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[1.42262pt]
\sigma_{d}(u_{p,\ell})&0&\ \forall d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.\qquad\text{ and }\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{\;}r@{\;=\;}l@{\;\;}l@{}}\mu_{0}\Delta u_{1,0}+\omega^{2}\rho_{0}u_{1,0}&0&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
u_{1,0}&0&\text{ on }\Sigma_{\Omega}\\[1.42262pt]
u_{1,0}&\frac{\mu_{0}}{\mu_{1}}\partial_{y}u_{0,0}&\text{ on }\Gamma\\[1.42262pt]
\sigma_{d}(u_{1,0})&0&\ \forall d\in-\frac{\pi}{\Theta}\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{array}\right.

4
Error estimates

Let
\chi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be equal to 1 on
B(0,1)
and 0 outside
B(0,2)
, and, for any
\eta>0
,
\chi_{\eta}:x\mapsto\chi(\frac{x}{\eta})
. We denote
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}:=(-\varepsilon,1)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
and
\Pi_{\varepsilon}:=\Omega\sqcup\Gamma\sqcup\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
(defined similarly as
\Pi
at page
2
). We define on
\Pi_{\varepsilon}
the following variant of the far-and-layer fields
\mathbf{u}_{p,\ell}

\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{p,\ell}(x,y):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}u_{p,\ell}(x,y)&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
U_{p,\ell}(x,\frac{y}{\varepsilon})&\text{ in }\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\end{array}\right.

We also denote, for any
p\in\mathbb{P}
,
n_{p}:=\max\{\ell\in\mathbb{N}\mid u_{p,\ell}\neq 0\text{ or }U_{p,\ell}\neq 0\text{ or }S_{p,\ell}\neq 0\}
.

Let us define the approximate global field at order
P\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
as follows for any
\varepsilon
small enough

\forall(x,y)\in\Omega_{\varepsilon},\quad u_{\varepsilon,P}(x,y):=(1-\chi_{\eta}(x,y))\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathbb{P}\cap[0,P]\\
\ell\in[\![0,n_{p}]\!]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{p,\ell}(x,y)\;+\;\chi_{\eta}(x,y)\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\in\mathbb{P}\cap[0,P]\\
\ell\in[\![0,n_{p}]\!]\end{subarray}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,S_{p,\ell}({\textstyle\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{y}{\varepsilon}})

where
\eta:=\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. Note that
(1-\chi_{\eta}(x,y))\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{p,\ell}(x,y)
is well-defined on
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
when
\eta>\varepsilon R_{\mathrm{c}}
since
\Pi_{\varepsilon}\setminus B(0,\varepsilon R_{\mathrm{c}})=\Omega_{\varepsilon}\setminus B(0,\varepsilon R_{\mathrm{c}})
. We will see as a consequence of Theorem
4
that
u_{\varepsilon,P}\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
.

The matching zone is
\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap C_{\eta}
where
C_{\eta}
is the annulus
C_{\eta}:=B(0,2\eta)\setminus B(0,\eta)
. Letting
\eta=\sqrt{\varepsilon}
makes the matching zone tend to 0 w.r.t. the far fields (because
\eta\rightarrow 0
when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
) and to infinity w.r.t. the corner fields (because
\frac{\eta}{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\infty
). Thanks to the matching assumption, we can state the following first error estimate concercing the error in the matching zone. We use the symbol
\lesssim
for majorations valid up to a constant independent of
\varepsilon
.

Lemma 4.1:
Let
P\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. For
\varepsilon
small enough, we have

\bigg\|\sum_{p\in\mathbb{P}\cap[0,P]}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,\big(\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{p,\ell}(x,y)-S_{p,\ell}({\textstyle\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{y}{\varepsilon}})\big)\bigg\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap C_{\eta})}\lesssim\varepsilon^{\frac{P}{2}-1}.

.

Proof:
We will compare
\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{p,\ell}
and
S_{p,\ell}
in
C_{\eta}
to their asymptotic expansions at 0, resp
\infty
. Let us denote:

•

\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon,P}:=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{P}\cap[0,P]}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,\mathbf{u}^{\varepsilon}_{p,\ell}
in
\Pi_{\varepsilon}

•

S_{\varepsilon,P}:=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{P}\cap[0,P]}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\,S_{p,\ell}({\textstyle\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{y}{\varepsilon}})
in
\Omega_{\varepsilon}

•

\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon,P}^{\mathcal{A}}:=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{P}\cap[0,P]}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\cdot\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}l}T_{\leqslant P-p}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})(x,y)&\text{ in }\Omega\\[1.42262pt]
T_{\leqslant P-p}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})(x,{\textstyle\frac{y}{\varepsilon}})&\text{ in }\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\end{array}\right.

•

S_{\varepsilon,P}^{\mathcal{A}}:=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{P}\cap[0,P]}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}\varepsilon^{p}\ln^{\ell}\!\varepsilon\cdot T_{\geqslant p-P}(S^{\infty}_{p,\ell})({\textstyle\frac{x}{\varepsilon},\frac{y}{\varepsilon}})
in
\Pi_{\varepsilon}
.

(see Definition
2.4
for
T_{\bullet}
). We split the estimate into three parts that we will majorize separately:

\|\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon,P}-S_{\varepsilon,P}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap C_{\eta})}\leqslant\|\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon,P}-\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon,P}^{\mathcal{A}}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap C_{\eta})}+\|\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon,P}^{\mathcal{A}}-S_{\varepsilon,P}^{\mathcal{A}}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap C_{\eta})}+\|S_{\varepsilon,P}^{\mathcal{A}}-S_{\varepsilon,P}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap C_{\eta})}.

In addition, we will split some of the norms
\|{\dots}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon}\cap C_{\eta})}
into
\|{\dots}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega\cap C_{\eta})}+\|{\dots}\|_{H^{1}(\Lambda_{\varepsilon}\cap C_{\eta})}
.

1.

\|\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon,P}-\mathbf{u}_{\varepsilon,P}^{\mathcal{A}}\|_{H^{1}(\Omega\cap C_{\eta})}
:
By Proposition
3.3
, for any
(p,\ell)
we have
u_{p,\ell}-T_{\leqslant P-p}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})=o_{\partial}(r^{P-p})
in
\Omega
when
r\rightarrow 0
. By Definition
2.4
of
o_{\partial}
, it implies
u_{p,\ell}-T_{\leqslant P-p}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})=O(r^{P-p})=O(r^{P-p-1})
and
\nabla\big[u_{p,\ell}-T_{\leqslant P-p}(\mathbf{u}^{0}_{p,\ell})\big]=O(r^{P-p-1})
uniformly in
\theta
. Thus:
